A

KNIME and the Landscape of Tools for Business Analytics and Data Science

To keep up with the tremendous growth in the popularity of business analytics and data science that we have been experiencing in recent years (both in academia and industry), the tools used to implement these technologies have been evolving and expanding at an unprecedented pace. As shown in Figure 2.4 in Chapter 2, “Introduction to Predictive Analytics and Data Mining,” there are numerous software tools, ranging from commercial/paid to free/open source, from graphical workflow-type to language-based programming platforms. Some of these tools are cloud based, some are individual local installations, and some are both, providing local tools and complementary cloud-based platforms for computational enhanced efficiency and ease of model deployment. Some of these tools are good at data preprocessing and/or flexible and powerful model building, and others are superior in terms of ease of learning, use, and deployment. As evidenced from the continually expending landscape of tool offerings, there is no one tool that is best at everything. Most business analytics and data science professionals tend to use a portfolio of tools and programming languages collectively to produce the best possible outcomes for their data analytics projects.

The success of data scientists at work depends largely on the tools they rely on. A data scientist needs to have mathematical and statistical knowledge of the underlying algorithms, experience with the most effective analytics platforms, domain knowledge/wisdom, and team/people management skills. However, there are other more contingent and complementary factors that also influence the final success and the impression left by a data science project on the stakeholders.

Clearly, every project has a deadline, a list of requirements, and a budget to meet. Most data mining projects are designed so stringently that there is not much room for sandboxing or an extended trial-and-error approach. A data scientist is expected to identify and implement a solution in a short time and make sure it is implemented correctly—that is, that it meets the project requirements. To do so, the data scientist may need to quickly experiment with different techniques to identify and adopt the best possible roadmap and repeated mechanisms for the project. Of course, every project has a budget, too. The fast implementation of the right solution is often additionally constrained by a limited budget.

Some projects are quite complex and require specialized algorithms beyond just the classic general machine learning algorithms. Sometimes data scientists are forced to learn new techniques and new algorithms on the spot and quickly to meet the deadline. The more time a data scientist has to dedicate to theory and math, the more quickly the learning will proceed.

Finally, the passage from prototype to production must be as fast (again) and as secure as possible. A data scientist cannot degrade the parts of the solution while moving it into production or risk exposing it to less secure execution, considering that the solution will be consumed by a different set of less expert users. If and when required, a different set of solutions—with more or less interaction and sophistication and with a more secure framework—can and should be made available for the consumption of the larger public.

Many other contingent factors—such as ease of learning, speed of prototype implementation, debugging and testing options to ensure the correctness of the solution, flexibility to experiment with different approaches, availability of help from external contributors and experts, and automation and security capabilities—contribute to the success of the data science project beyond the math, the experience, and the domain knowledge. All these contingent factors depend heavily on the tool and the data science platform the data scientists choose to use.

I have used many of the analytics platforms, commercial and free/open source for educational (teaching business analytics data science courses at every level, undergraduate, masters, doctoral, and executive education) and commercial (consulting to for-profit companies, nonprofit organization, and government agencies) purposes in my 30-plus years in the field. Although I still use a variety of analytics tools and data science platforms, for over 6 years now, I have been using KNIME as my primary analytics platform. With KNIME, I have been able to create and deploy data science solutions for complex problems that have resulted in value-creating outcomes for my clients, high-impact publications in reputable journals, and case studies for my textbooks. One particular feature of KNIME that makes it very attractive for me is its open and expandable architecture, which enables me to incorporate the capabilities of popular data science tools and programming languages, including Python, R, Spark, and H2O, into the workflow in a seamless manner. The following sections describe the various reasons KNIME works well as an analytics platform.

Project Constraints: Time and Money

KNIME Analytics Platform is open source software for all your data needs. It is free to download from www.knime.com/downloads and free to use—not only for learning and education but for commercial use too. As a free open source product, KNIME reduces the licensing related legal headaches and the impact on the project budget.

The download, which is the full version of the software (not a restricted community version, covers a rich collection of data wrangling and machine learning techniques and is based on visual programming. Visual programming has become quite popular recently as an alternative to traditional coding. In visual programming, a graphical user interface (GUI) guides you through all the necessary steps to build a pipeline (workflow) of dedicated blocks (nodes). Each node implements a given task; each workflow of nodes takes your data from the beginning to the end of the designed journey. A workflow replaces a traditional script, and a node replaces one or more script lines.

In KNIME Analytics Platform, you create nodes by dragging and dropping (or double-clicking) from the Node Repository into the workflow editor in the central part of the KNIME workbench. Node after node, in a logical order, you build the pipeline. Figure A.1 shows the KNIME Analytics Platform graphical user interface, which includes the following components (clockwise from the top-left corner):

  • KNIME Explorer to store the workflows

  • Workflow Coach for recommendations

  • Node Repository as the node storage

  • Outline for the overview

  • Console for the errors

  • Description to learn more

  • The workflow editor (in the middle) to assemble the workflow

Visual programming allows for quick prototyping, as well as ease of explaining and understanding the underlying logic of the project. It makes KNIME very easy to learn and use by less-expert consumers of business analytics. You can quickly produce a few different experimental prototypes before deciding the final direction of the project. The ease of implementation frees up time to think more deeply about possible theoretical alternatives to the current solution. I, as an instructor, like the fact that it makes my job easier to explain the complete process of converting data into actionable insight in a logical/orderly fashion with the help of an intuitive graphical workflow.

The Learning Curve

Visual programming has a less steep learning curve than code-based tools. Data science is now used in more or less all disciplines, including the humanities, languages, life sciences, economics, social sciences, and engineering. Not all scientists (or managers/decision makers) are expert programmers, and not all of them have enough spare time to become expert programmers. A GUI-based visual tool can be learned and applied in much less time than a code-based tool, freeing up precious time and resources for more important investigations, conceptualizations, and problem solving.

Also, those who are preparing to become future scientists in the humanities, languages, life sciences, economics, or other disciplines appreciate the ease of use a GUI-based tool. The idea is to shorten the time spent on syntactic details of programming in order to spend more quality time on the semantics of model building and problem solving. I have too often seen entire months dedicated to learning the syntactic nature of coding practices before any data analysis techniques are approached or implemented. With KNIME Analytics Platform, in just a few weeks, you can assemble quite capable workflows for data transformation and for the training and testing of a wide variety of machine learning algorithms. That said, for uniquely complex and novel projects, a data scientist might need to incorporate some unique and advanced functionalities that exist only in analytics programming languages, such as Python and R, into the model building and problem-solving process. Because it is built on an open and extendable architecture, KNIME Analytics Platform also allows for those type of extensions through a selection of scripting and wrapper nodes.

Plenty of educational resources about KNIME Analytics Platform are available on the Internet (especially on the KNIME site, of course) to help you get going quickly. Starting from the generic LEARNING page (https://www.knime.com/resources) on the KNIME site, you can move on to courses with an instructor (https://www.knime.com/learning/events) or completely self-taught courses (https://www.knime.com/knime-introductory-course), and you can eventually obtain certification for the platform. You may also decide to learn by yourself with the help of a book, like this one.

Help for beginners is also available at the KNIME Hub (https://hub.knime.com/), where the creators of the platform have expanded the already existing set of resource for KNIME Analytics Platform within the KNIME community.

The KNIME Community

The KNIME Hub is the public repository for the KNIME community. Here, you can share your workflows and download workflows from other KNIME users. Just type in the keywords, and you get a list of related workflows, components, extensions, and more. It is a great place to start with plenty of examples! For example, enter “basic” or “beginners” in the search box, and you get a list of sample workflows illustrating basic concepts in KNIME Analytics Platform; type in “read file,” and you get a list of sample workflows illustrating how to read CSV files, table files, Excel files, and so on. (Note that some of these sample workflows are also reported in the EXAMPLES server in the KNIME Explorer in the top-left corner of the KNIME workbench.)

When you isolate a workflow that you might be interested in, you can click on it to open its page and then download it or open it in KNIME Analytics Platform. In your local workspace, you can start adapting the workflow to your data and your needs. After searching for ready-to-use pieces of code, you can just download, reuse, and adapt workflows or pieces of workflows from the KNIME Hub as appropriate for your own problem.

Of course, you can also share your own work on the KNIME Hub for the public good. Just copy the workflows to share from your local workspace into the My-KNIME-Hub/Public folder in the KNIME Explorer panel (see Figure A.2).

The KNIME community does not stop at the KNIME Hub. You can get tips and tricks on the KNIME Forum (https://forum.knime.com/) as well. Here, you can ask questions or search for previous answers. The community is very active, and it is highly likely that somebody has already asked your question.

Finally, contributions by the community are available as posts on the KNIME Blog (https://www.knime.com/blog), as books in KNIME Press (https://www.knime.com/knimepress), and as videos on KNIMETV (https://www.youtube.com/user/KNIMETV) channel on YouTube.

Correctness and Flexibility

Easy is good, but correctness is paramount. You need to know that a tool is flexible enough to experiment with alternative methods and procedures. This is indeed a key issue because for many of the software tools available today, easy is available only if you sacrifice control and flexibility. Many tools seem like canned, black-box solutions. For a data scientist, such magical solutions have always been questionable. It is important for a data scientist to have full control of the semi-experimental process and its underlying options to finding the most optimal solutions.

Automated machine learning (also called AutoML or AML) has become very popular recently. It carries the promise of getting your data and spitting out some results without requiring you to lift a finger. As fascinating as this option might sound, it carries some risk. First of all, it works as a black box: Its decision process is not transparent. When I feed the box with my data, I have to completely trust the machine that the data analysis process is correct, fits the data characteristics and distributions, and is tuned on the problem I wish to solve. I, personally, prefer to make sure that all steps in the analysis are implemented according to best practices, under my control, and as appropriate to the original design of the application. Although AutoML is a desired final stage in data science, because of the artistic nature of the solution development, it is still a work in progress, a utopia, and for real data scientist, it is nothing more than an early experimental step in piloting.

When executing a node in KNIME Analytics Platform, you can inspect the node options and output results (by simply right-clicking and then selecting the last option in the context menu) for debugging and validating the application step by step and make sure that all steps work as required by design. The world is never perfect/ideal, and neither are the data that comes out of it. Automated analysis might work well on perfect data that contains no unbalanced classes, no outliers, no dirty records, and so on. But perfect data only comes in fake data sets. In real life, there are always adjustments to make for the unbalanced classes, for the dirty data, for the missing values, for the outliers and inconsistencies, and so on. Adding one cleaning step or another, introducing an optimization cycle on one parameter or another might change the final results of the workflow. In order to experiment with new strategies, it is imperative that the tool be flexible and customizable enough at each step of the analysis. A black-box automated approach does not allow for much flexibility, does not allow you to change intermediate steps in the analysis, to customize features, or to tune parameters. KNIME, on the other hand, is very modular and, with extensive coverage of data wrangling operations and machine learning algorithms, it allows for ample flexibility; it is flexible enough that you can exchange one data manipulation node with another with a simple drag-and-drop, introduce an optimization loop on the fly, or systematically and easily change/optimize the value of a parameter in the training of a specific machine learning model.

Note that if you open a workflow developed on an older version of KNIME Analytics Platform, you will probably find deprecated or legacy nodes. Current use of these nodes is discouraged as better, newer nodes have likely been developed. However, deprecated and legacy nodes do still exist, and they do still work. Indeed, backward compatibility with previous versions is a key and highly desired feature of analytics platforms like KNIME. Ensuring that old workflows still work exactly the same way they were designed to work can guarantee the reproducibility of the results. As you know, this is an extremely important argument for the validation of results in scientific research.

Extensive Coverage of Data Science Techniques

Another necessary complement to the ease of use in an analytics tool is the coverage of data science techniques. Ease of use would be of limited convenience without extensive coverage of data wrangling techniques, machine learning algorithms, handlers of a wide range of data types and formats, and integration with the most commonly used database software and data sources, reporting tools, and other scripting languages. A capable analytics platform is expected to keep up with the latest trends and technologies through native implementations and/or seamless external connections. Since no tool can do it all, all the time, integrating with and playing nicely with other tools and technologies in the fast-evolving business analytics data science landscape is not just a nice-to-have capability but a requirement for a comprehensive analytics platform.

Machine Learning Algorithms

KNIME Analytics Platform covers most machine learning algorithms—from traditional ones (e.g., linear and logistic regression, decision trees, neural networks, support vector machines, Naïve Bayes, k-nearest neighbor) to more recent ensembles (e.g., random forest, gradient boosted trees) and deep learning algorithms, from recommendation engines to a number of clustering techniques. Most of these algorithms are native in KNIME Analytics Platform, and some are integrated from other open source libraries through scripting nodes. One specific example is the deep learning capabilities, where layers, units, and preconfigured architectures are made available through the KNIME Deep Learning–Keras integration (https://www.knime.com/deeplearning/keras). This integration allows for easy use of the Keras libraries within KNIME Analytics Platform by offering them to the end users via the familiar KNIME graphical workflow-type user interface. It is possible to drag and drop nodes to create neuron layers for complex deep neural network architectures and train and test the final network without the need to write a single line of code. Figure A.3 shows a snapshot of the machine learning techniques included in KNIME.

Rich Collection of Data Handlers

A number of KNIME nodes are available to implement a myriad of data wrangling techniques. By logically connecting and combining individual nodes dedicated to small/atomic data processing tasks, you can implement very complex data preprocessing and transformation operations. The assembling of such operations is so easy that KNIME is often used for data preparation for other tools, such as to generate a consolidated data set for Excel or the data for creating reports in Tableau or PowerBI or to create summarized and synthesized data to be pushed into a data warehouse.

KNIME Analytics Platform also connects to most of the commonly used data sources: from databases to cloud repositories, from big data platforms to individual flat files. So no matter how weird your data source and your data types might be, it is quite likely that you can connect to them and consume them from within KNIME Analytics Platform.

Community Extensions and Third-party Integrations

What if all that is not enough? What if you need a specific procedure for DNA analysis or molecule conversion? What if you need a specific network manipulation function from Python? What if you need to export your results into a highly specialized web report? Where KNIME Analytics Platform cannot reach with its native nodes, there is usually at least one third-party extension from the KNIME community that provides the missing functionality for that particular domain specification and the related data type. Furthermore, there are also the integrations with other scripting and programming languages, such as Python, R, Java, and JavaScript, to consume additional functionality of other libraries and community resources.

KNIME Analytics Platform allows for seamless integration with the BIRT reporting tool, which is also open source. Integrations with other reporting platforms, such as Tableau, QlikView, PowerBI, and Spotfire, are also available. However, in those cases, you would need to buy the necessary license and install the destination reporting software.

In most cases, it turns out that it is not even necessary to move to an external reporting software tool to visualize your data and your analysis results. A number of JavaScript-based nodes are already available within KNIME, in the Views/JavaScript category in the Node repository. These nodes implement data visualization using a variety of plots and charts—from a simple scatterplot to a more complex sunburst chart, from a simple histogram to a parallel coordinate plot, and more. These nodes seem simple but are potentially quite powerful. If you combine them within a component (i.e., an integrated modeling construct in KNIME), you can enable interactive selection of data points across multiple charts. The component thus inherits and combines all views from the contained nodes and connects them together such that, if points are selected and visualized in one chart, they can also be selected and visualized in the other charts of the component’s composite view. For example, the three plots in Figure A.4 visualize the same data and are connected so that, when you select data in the bar chart, you also select and can optionally visualize only the selected data in the other two charts.

Data Science in the Enterprise

One last step is to deploy the outputs of the modeling efforts into production so that the insight generated by these models can be consumed for better and faster decision making. In an enterprise, this deployment must be quick, easy/intuitive, seamless, and secure. This is the last and perhaps the most critical step in the chain of actions in a data science project. When the workflow is finished (i.e., the model is trained and tested and the performance is measured), you need to push the application out into the real world to deal with real-life data to provide value to the intended user base. This process of moving the application into the real world is called moving into production. The process of including the model in this final application is called deployment. These phases are deeply connected and can be quite problematic because all errors in the application design can and will show up at this stage.

It is possible to move an application into production by using KNIME Analytics Platform. If you, as a lone data scientist or a data science student, do not regularly deploy applications and models, KNIME Analytics Platform is probably enough for your needs. However, if you are just a bit more in an enterprise kind of environment, where scheduling, versioning, access rights, and all other typical functions of a production server are needed, then just using KNIME Analytics Platform for production can be cumbersome and may not produce sufficient value to the enterprise. In such a case, the KNIME Server, which is not open source but sold for an annual license fee, can make your life much easier. First, this server-based deployment fits better with the governance of the enterprise’s IT environment. In addition, it offers a protected collaboration environment for your group and the whole data science lab. Finally, it makes model deployment and moving into production easier, faster, and safer, thanks to KNIME’s integrated deployment feature and one-click deployment into production. End users can run the application from a KNIME Analytics Platform client or, even better, from a web-based application from within a browser.

The composite views mentioned earlier, which offer interactive and interconnected views of selected points, become fully formed web pages and easy-to-use applications when the solution is executed on a web browser via the KNIME Server’s web portal. By using the components as touchpoints within the workflow, you get a guided analytics (https://www.knime.com/knime-introductory-course/chapter1/section4/guided-analytics) application within the web browser. The end user can take advantage of these touchpoints to insert knowledge or preferences and to steer the analysis in the desired direction.

Summary and Conclusions

With the brief introduction in this appendix, I hope that I have convinced you to give KIME a try and see for yourself why KNIME Analytics Platform is an excellent choice as a business analytics and data science tool to support you in your future endeavors as a data scientist.

KNIME’s ease of use will save you time and allow you to dedicate more of your brain and energy to more important research topics. And remember that it is free, and solid community support it is available. KNIME also offers debugging capability that allows you to check the correctness of the implemented operations and remember the atomic tasks for all nodes so you can easily experiment with different analysis strategies. The easy prototyping is indeed one of the best features of KNIME Analytics Platform, enabling quick evaluation of and experimentation with new techniques.

Machine learning algorithms, data wrangling techniques, and accessible data sources make KNIME Analytics Platform a very reliable tool, but it is also easy to use. If the native nodes and extensions are not enough, community extensions, integrations with other scripting and programming languages, and integration with reporting tools can make up for the missing domain-specific functionalities.

Finally, the use of the KNIME Server as a complement to KNIME Analytics Platform for the enterprise allows for easy and secure deployment and seamless integration in the company IT environment.

Table A.1 summarizes the main reasons to consider KNIME Analytics Platform as a potential tool for your data science learning, teaching, and execution, using a top-10 list analogy.
